The step came first. She pushed off the line, a curb's height and a pace, and shut the Ember at the top, and set the still door under her foot before the ground. It was like stepping onto a mattress the size of the yard. Orla's ferrule came against her shoulder and went through her and into the earth, and she counted three and gave the ground its weight back and shut the door.

"Clean," said Orla. "Is the thread cold?"

Kiva laid two fingers of her right hand on her breastbone.

"Cold."

"Then you may have your go. To the cross. One low bound, as it was in the spring, and not an inch higher because there are people looking." The stick pointed. "Say it."

"Rowan, changing on three. Ember, to still."

It sounded very small. She had last said it across an arena to eleven hundred people. There were four here, and a brass head on three legs, and a post that leaned.

The mast ticked behind her. She had her back to it, on Orla's terms.

"*One.*"

The metal came off the cuff and ran down her sides and closed on her calves. She bent both knees, the good and the newly passed, and left the line.

It was four paces. It was nothing. She had crossed a brick sump on the same push with a Flask at her back.

And she could hear the pen.

She had not thought of that. On the shelf there had been a roar in the terraces and her own blood. Here there was a quiet yard, and behind her left shoulder a steel nib that had been scratching evenly and was now, as the struts took her weight, running up its paper with a small, thin, eager sound. She knew what it was drawing. She knew to the hundredth.

"*Two.*"

She shut the Ember at the top.

She shut it, and listened to hear whether the pen had heard.

It was no more than that. It was a quarter of a breath. But it was the same fault that she had spent a month unlearning with a tin and a stick, when she used to watch the metal run home along her arm to make certain it had arrived. The struts let go late. One of them clung for an instant, and she felt it cling. The metal was still running up her ribs when the top of the bound was already behind her and the cross was coming up out of the dirt.

She had half a breath in which to choose.

The still door was there. It was ready; she could feel it under her as she felt a pan under ash. If she opened it now it would open on a doorway that was not yet empty, and she knew precisely what that was called, and she had done it four times in this yard and once in a cart shed.

*Is the Ember shut clean at the top?*

*No.*

"*Off!*" said Kiva. "Off, off—"

She let go of the pan. She let the whole of it go, and came down as nothing but a girl of seventeen travelling a good deal too fast, and she took the ground on her right foot and did not try to keep it. She folded at the hip as she had been shown and went over her own shoulder, and the Old Yard came round once and hit her in the back.

She lay a pace short of the cross and half a pace to the side of it, looking up at the lamp.

Nothing hurt. That was the first thing. The left knee had not been asked to do anything. There was dirt in her collar and her right elbow was skinned, and the bottom thread on her breastbone was as cold as it had been on the line.

"Hand," said Orla's voice, above her.

Kiva held up her left hand. The old fingers turned it over in the lamplight, and pressed the two tips, and let go.

"Pink. Good." The crooked face came into view against the sky. "Now your face, since you're so anxious I should have it. You're angry."

"I was listening to the pen."

"I know you were. I watched your head go." Orla straightened, with a grunt. "And then you did the only thing in sixteen goes that I've set you and you've done. You asked yourself the question at the top, you got the wrong answer, and you came down on it. I've seen that ordered a great many times. I've seen it obeyed about twice."

"It's another miss."

"It's the first thing you've done in this yard that I'd have paid for, and it turns out that somebody has." She looked over her shoulder. "Mr. Bexley. She's finished."

Noa came out from behind the mast with the tape across both palms. He was trying very hard to keep his face from saying anything, and not succeeding.

"The step. Two hundredths; it always is. Then the bound, two again. Then there's a tremble at the top, about as long as a blink, and a hundredth goes on it. And then nothing. It's flat. You shut everything." He looked up. "Five. Five hundredths for the night, and three of them were the go."

"Three hundredths," said Kellan. He had his own book out, and he wrote it under a ruled line. "One dram and ninety-seven left. I'd call that cheap."

"I'd wanted the cross," said Kiva. She had sat up, and she was looking at it. It was a pace away, scratched in the dirt, and she had never yet come down on it in that yard. "I could feel the door. It was there."

"I'm sure it was," said Perrin. "I'd my mouth open. I'd got as far as the *o.*" He lowered himself onto the spare crate with great care. "I'd like you to know that I've never been so pleased to be beaten to a word in my life. I'm putting it on the list of things I don't mind."

She laughed, which she had not meant to do, and found that the anger had a crack in it.

She got up by herself. She brushed off what dirt would come, and went and stood on the cross for a moment in her ordinary boots, with nothing open, to see what the yard looked like from there. It looked much the same. The post leaned over her left shoulder. Under her shirt the disk lay against her breastbone, cold as a coin, and she noticed that she had not thought of it once between the line and the ground.

"Friday," said Orla. "Not tomorrow. Tomorrow you'll come and sit with your back to that drum for an hour while Bexley runs it empty, until you can't tell it from a clock." She was already turning toward the shed. "You'll want to write it. I'd put it short."

Kiva wrote it under the lamp, on her knee, while the others packed the drum.

*Sanctioned go, the first. Old Yard, travelling mast. Called off at the top. Why: the pen. I listened for it, like the metal. The door was ready and the doorway wasn't.*

She thought. Then, in the column that asked what it had cost:

*0.03. An elbow. Nothing else.*

And beneath that, because she had been asked for it on a pavement at a quarter to six in the morning by somebody who would know anyway:

*I'd an answer ready for Orla before I'd stood up. She had hers first.*

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-28.md

# Chapter 28 — Kiva: The Cheap One

On Thursday she sat with her back to the drum for an hour, and by the end of it she could not have told the pen from the lamp.

It was the dullest hour she had ever spent in the Old Yard. Noa ran the tape empty, and Kiva sat on the spare crate six paces off with the field book shut, and Orla read a newspaper. At first every scratch of the nib went up her spine. Then it was only a sound, like the hiss of the wick, and somewhere about the fortieth minute she found that she was thinking of how a stiff new sheet might be got to lie flat, and had been for some time.

"That'll do," Orla had said. "You've stopped sitting up for it."

On Friday night she stood on the scraped line with the thread cold and the step behind her, and said the words to four people and a post.

"*One.*"

The struts closed. She left the line.

There was the nib, running up its paper. It ran, and it was a clock.

"*Two.*"

She shut the Ember at the top, and this time she did not wait to learn whether anybody had heard. The struts let go together. The metal went home along her ribs like water into a drain, and it arrived, and she had nothing.

She had forgotten how much nothing it was.

On the shelf there had been a wall coming, and copper under her, and no room to notice. Here she noticed. For as long as it takes to say a short word she was a girl in the air with no doors, and all the speed of the bound in her body, and she felt the ash go. It did not trickle. It went out of her all at once, a quarter of a dram, like the breath out of somebody who has been thumped between the shoulders, and it went nowhere that she could follow.

*That's the price,* she thought. *That's what he drew.*

She set the pan.

She set it under herself and ahead, wide and waiting, on a patch of dirt with two scratches in it.

"*Three.*"

Her right foot came down on the cross.

There was no jar. The yard took her as a great hand takes a small weight, and the bound went in at her heel and down, through the packed earth and whatever five hundred years of feet had made of it. The left knee never knew.

"Four," she said, and did not put the lid on.

Orla's ferrule came against her left shoulder. Orla leaned on it. She was a heavy woman and she leaned as she would have leaned on a door, and it went through Kiva like a rumor and into the ground.

"Five."

"Hold it."

"Six. Seven." There was a small bright thing rising in her chest. She knew what that was as well. She gave it a number. "Eight."

"Give it back," said Orla.

Kiva gave the ground its weight, slowly, as someone sets down a full pan, and shut the still door behind it with nothing left in the doorway.

Then she stood on two scratches in the dirt, in her ordinary boots, and shook.

"Hand."

She held it out. The fingers were pink. The first two were cold, and no colder than they had been at breakfast.

"Thread?"

"Cold. It never warmed."

"No. I was watching your collar." Orla took her stick back under her own weight. She did not smile. She looked at the cross, and at the boots on it, and at the line four paces off. "I've sat on that crate since the seventh of last month waiting to see a foot come down there. I'd begun to think the mark was badly placed."

"Is that one?"

"That's one." The crooked face was perfectly grave. "I saw a shelf from the far side of a pit, over a rail, and I've wondered every day since whether the place was being kind to you. It seems not. Now show me it wasn't the yard." She turned. "Mr. Bexley."

Noa read it with his thumbs on the tape. Two hundredths for the bound. Twenty-five in less than a fifth of a second, straight up, with the same small blot where the nib had jarred. Three for the stance.

"Thirty," he said. His voice cracked on it. "A dram and sixty-seven left."

"Sixty-seven," said Kellan, writing. He had gone rather pale. "I knew what it would cost. I proposed it. I hadn't watched it go."

"I'd like to say that I mind," said Perrin, from the other crate.

"We know."

"I'd like to say it anyway. That's near half a Kennel floor, gone in the time it takes me to sneeze." He was grinning from ear to ear. "And she came down like a cat on a cushion, and I held a capstan with my ribs for less. Write it down that I'm furious."

Kiva's hand had gone to her collar. She had not sent it. The disk was there under the cloth, round and plain, and it was as cold as the inside of a well.

It had been warm once. It had been warm for the first one, for the length of a road stair, and she had not asked it why. It did not seem that the second was to be told apart from a wet Tuesday.

She found that she had expected that. She let go of the cord, and it was not until she was writing up the columns that she noticed she had not been disappointed, and wondered a little at how much quieter a thing was when nobody was waiting on its opinion.

---

The third team went to Stonehand on Saturday and lost by two.

She was let play. Aske had said lanes, and Orla had said one door and no cleverness, and so for three carries Kiva stood on the Long Terrace with her still door declared and her heel on a riser and did nothing whatever that would have interested a gallery. Idony Keld feinted at her knee eleven times. Pru leaned a plank from the porch to the sill and talked to it. The litter went the wrong way twice.

It finished eight to six against them. Nobody fell over in an interesting manner, and afterward, in the passage under the terrace, Lowen Brask was waiting with his coat on and a canvas roll at his feet.

"I go out on Monday," he said. "A Home Line hold crew, till the autumn. Vane asked, and I've said yes. So that's the last you'll see of me in a lane this season, and I thought I'd say so, since it's twice now that I've had you and once that I haven't."

"Once was enough."

"For you. I've been turning it over ever since." He was a square, quiet young man, and he looked at her as he had looked at her from a gallery, with a notebook. "Cade tells me you're doing it on a mast. He tells everybody. He minds it very loudly."

"He does."

"Is it still the right that opens late?"

"It opens before, now. That's all it ever wanted. Somebody told me to arrive before I was there."

"Then somebody taught you my trade behind my back." He nearly smiled. "I'll give you one more for nothing, since I shan't be here to take it off you in a lane. I've had the junior lanes three springs. Every spring there's one who finds a landing easy, a week before the others. I used to think that was talent." He picked up the roll. "It's nearly always a child standing on two things at once and not knowing it. A step and a rail. A foot and a friend. So when it comes easy, look at your feet."

"It doesn't come easy."

"Not yet," said Lowen Brask, and went up the passage toward the light.

She wrote it down that night, as she wrote everything. She put it under Saturday, in the left-hand column, as a thing that somebody had said. She did not see that it wanted a place on the right.

---

Monday's go was the best that she had ever made.

She knew it in the air. The new week's thimble was in her, and four-tenths over, and the step had been clean, and the thread was cold when she said so. She had slept. She had not listened for the pen since Thursday.

"*One.*"

She left the line as she might have left a curb.

It was the third time in six days that she had gone up over that strip of dirt, and something in her had begun to know the road. On Wednesday she had fought the top of it. On Friday she had gone through it like a diver through cold water, with her teeth shut. Tonight she came to the top, and the Ember was closing, and she found that there was no need to wait in the dark for it to finish.

"*Two.*"

She could feel exactly where the ground would be. She could feel the pan wanting to be set. And the metal was nearly home, all but the last of it, a thread along the ribs, and it seemed the most natural thing in the world to begin the one as the other ended. It was how one hand gives a cup to the other. She did not set anything down.

There was no nothing.

There was no girl in the air without a door. The breath was not knocked out of her. One thing ran into the next as smoothly as a seam, and the cross came up, and her foot came down on it.

"*Three.*"

It was soft. It was softer than Friday. She stood on the cross and the whole yard stood under her, and when Orla's ferrule came against her shoulder it went through her so easily that she could have laughed.

"Four. Five. Six."

"Hold."

"Seven. Eight."

"Give it back."

She gave it back, and shut the door, and this time she was not shaking. She stood with her hands at her sides, breathing as if she had walked up a flight of stairs.

"Well," said Orla slowly. The stick came back to the ground. "I felt nothing wrong in that. I'd my whole weight on you and there was nothing there to find." She was frowning a little, as a person frowns at a sum that has come out too neatly. "Hand."

Pink. Both fingers. Not even cold.

"Thread?"

Kiva laid two fingers on her breastbone.

She had known before they touched. She had known in the air, and had put it by, because the cross was coming and the cross was what she was there for.

It was warm. It was only a little warm, like a cup an hour after the tea, and it was going as she felt it.

"A little," she said. "It's nearly gone."

Orla did not move.

"When?"

"In the air. At the top." Her mouth had gone dry. "It wasn't a jam. I'd know a jam; I've had five. Nothing fought. I thought it was only that I was pleased."

"You were pleased before you'd landed?"

"I was—it was easy. It was the first one that's ever been easy."

"And the meter said *one,* and you didn't."

There was nothing to say to that. On the spare crate, Perrin had stopped smiling. He was looking at his own hands.

"I never called it," he said. "I'd nothing to call. It looked better than Friday's."

"It did," said Orla. "It looked better to me, and I've a stick. Mr. Bexley. She's finished. I'd be obliged if you'd read it out before anybody in this lane says another word, myself included."

Noa had the tape already. He had had it for some moments, and he was holding it very close to the lamp on the mast's leg, and his face was not that of someone with good news.

"The bound," he said. "Two hundredths. The stance, eight counts, three." He stopped.

"And between?" said Kellan.

"There isn't a between." Noa turned the tape so that they could see. "There's no cliff. Look. On Friday it goes straight up; you could cut yourself on it. Tonight there's a hump. It's about a third of a second across and it's six hundredths high, and it's got a round top." He swallowed. "Eleven. The whole go cost eleven hundredths."

For the space of a breath, nobody understood him.

Then Kellan made a sound.

"*Eleven,*" he said. "That's a third. That's a Draw of four, on the same work. Kiva, if you can do that twice, the two drams are eighteen goes, and Ysra Crest's bench is—" He had his pencil moving. His eyes were alight. "It's the gap that costs. You've found a way to close the gap."

"I didn't find it. It was just there."

"Then find it again. That's the best number anybody's—"

"No," said Noa.

It came out too loud. He went red to the hair, and he did not take it back.

"No. I'm sorry. It doesn't count, and I'd sooner say it now than let him work out what eighteen goes are worth." His hands were shaking, and he laid the tape down on the drum case and smoothed it flat. "I know this shape. I've drawn it before. It's on the Low Lane's tape for the thirteenth of last month, the eighth try, the one where she called *off* inside the breath and stood with her hands on her knees. Nine hundredths in less than a second. I said the pen went up like a mouse up a curtain. It had a round top."

"That was a jam," said Kiva.

"That was two doors drawing at once, and fighting. This is two doors drawing at once, and not." He looked at her, and she saw what it was costing him. "A crossing's a cliff because there's nothing open when it happens. That's what's so strange about it. That's the whole of my paper, if I ever write one. This isn't strange at all. It's a hump because for a third of a second there were two of them open together, and each of them was paying for itself in the ordinary way." His voice dropped. "I've wanted a Draw of four out of you since the fourth of last month. I'd have given a year for it. It isn't one. It's the other thing with good manners, and it's on a sanctioned tape, and I copy it for the Chair at nine tomorrow."

The mast ticked.

Kiva stood where she was. She could feel her own face, hot and stiff.

"I landed it," she said. "On the cross. She leaned on me with her whole weight and there was nothing to find. She said so."

"She did," said Orla.

"If it holds, and it's soft, and it costs a third—"

"Then it's what I put in front of four thousand people in 2661." Orla had not raised her voice. "Sit down, Rowan. Not because you're in disgrace. Because I'm about to tell you what you did, and I'd as soon you weren't standing on it."

Kiva sat on the cross. It was the nearest place.

"There are two ways to get from one door to another," said Orla. "I told you in the spring that I'd never seen the right one. I'd seen the wrong one a hundred times, in a dozen coats. You open the second before the first is shut. Do it badly and they fight, and you get a line on your chest and a post in your face, and that's a jam, and nobody mistakes it for anything. Do it well—" She stopped, and began again. "I never saw it done well. I didn't know it could be. I'd have told you an hour ago that a jam always throws."

"It didn't throw."

"No. It set you down like an egg. That's why it's worse." The stick came round and pointed, not at Kiva, but at the brass head on its three legs. "For a third of a second you were two things at once. In a dirt yard, with nothing near you but a post, that's a soft landing and eleven hundredths. Now put a charged cell three paces off. Put a sheet of open ash. You know what a door does to open ash; you've knelt beside enough of it. What do two do, together, in one body, with a seam between them that you can't feel?"

Kiva did not answer. She was remembering a drum under her left palm, and a needle that had gone on climbing after she took her hand away, and what had come up a chute to look for it.

"The Cellhouse," she said.

"That was three, and you were frightened, and every assessor in the building saw the line light. This was two, and you were happy, and your own sponsor would have signed it." Orla breathed out through her nose. "The gap's the price because the gap's the point. For the length of a word there's nothing of yours open for anything to get into. That's what you're buying with a quarter of a dram. It isn't waste. It's the wall between the rooms."

"A crossing," said Kellan quietly. He had stopped writing. He was looking at the eleven on his page as if it had been a stamp that said *First* on a tin of soot. "And that was a stack."

"That's as good a word as any."

"I'd have sworn to it. I'd have taken that number to the board." He drew a line under it, slowly, and wrote beside it. "My mother kept a jar for eleven months to prove that a figure was false. I had this one for about as long as it takes to sharpen a pencil. I'd like that entered. I was wrong faster than anybody here."

"You were wrong second," said Orla. "I was first, and I'd the stick." She turned to Noa, and for a moment she only looked at him: a narrow boy of seventeen with a strip of paper, who had just told a Torch of thirty years' standing that she could not count. "Mr. Bexley. I'd have put my name to that landing. I'd have written *two* and gone home pleased with the pair of us. You and your pen have kept me from the second great mistake of my life, and you did it against a number you wanted. I shan't forget it. I'll thank you not to let me."

Noa could not find anything to say. He nodded, and went on smoothing the tape.

"It goes in the book that Crest sees on Monday," said Orla. "As it was. *A stack, a third of a second, landed, nobody hurt. Called a miss by her reader before her sponsor had seen it. Thread warmed in the air; candidate did not call.* I'll not soften the last, and you'll not ask me to."

"No."

"What do I enter for why?"

It was the first time that Orla had ever asked her for that column aloud.

Kiva looked at her hands. She thought of a passage under a terrace, and a canvas roll, and a thing set down on the left side of a page because she had not seen that it belonged on the right.

"It was easy," she said. "And I was pleased. And I didn't look at my feet."

---

The card came down from the Brickfields road on Tuesday, by a boy in blue, and Kellan read it out in the drying yard.

"*Finch: a corner and a lamp. Not a wall. Sat. the 23rd, six p.m., the hall. Night van. Bring your sheet wet. R.S.*" He turned it over. There was nothing on the back. "That's the whole of it. She's used twenty-two words, and three of them are the date."

"What's a lamp?" said Pru.

"I should think it's what a ward is when it isn't asked to hold anything."

"I can do that. I do that most of the time." She took the card and read it herself, moving her lips. "*Not a wall.* She's underlined it. I've never been underlined by a Torch before. And what's the sheet to be wet for?"

Nobody knew. It was Mrs. Pryce who told them, from the scullery door, with her arms floured to the elbow.

"Because it's new, and it's waxed, and it'll stand up in ridges like a tent the first time you throw it down in a hurry. Anybody who's ever hung a tarpaulin could tell you. You want it trodden." She nodded at the long stone trough under the pump. "In there, with cold water, for an hour. Then it'll lie where it's put. Boots off."

So they trod it.

It took the three of them who had whole ribs, in the trough, in the last of the light, with their trousers rolled, marching up and down on six paces of waxed canvas like people pressing grapes. Perrin sat on the mounting block and called the step. The water was so cold that it hurt, and Kiva's left knee ached with a dull, honest ache that had nothing to do with landings.

"This is the sort of thing they don't put on the poster," said Pru. "You see a woman with a white streak in her hair and a blade as long as a street. Nobody shows you her feet."

"Tread," said Perrin. "Left. Left. Renn, you're out."

"I've been asked to a practice on Thursday," said Kellan.

He said it to the canvas. He went on treading.

"The Halden team. They train twice a week at the refinery yard, with a chartered foreman, on a contained string. Every week. They'd like to see me cut behind their ward line." His jaw was set. "I've said that I'll go and watch. I've not said anything else. I'm telling you because I said I'd tell you when the sum moved, and it's moved."

Nobody broke step.

"A contained string," said Perrin, after a moment. "Every week."

"Yes."

"We get Rhea Sorn for one night in a fortnight, and a trough." Perrin considered it. "I mind that for you, if you'd like. I've room."

"I'd not, particularly. Thank you."

"Then tread. Left."

Kiva said nothing. She had a name for what Kellan ought to do, and three reasons for it, and she was learning to know the weight of that in her mouth. She trod. The canvas was beginning to give under her heels, a little, like stiff dough.

"May I ask a thing?" said Pru, some while later. "Since we're all in a trough. I know there are four old lamps, and a clerk, and a box that costs something to open. I've decided to know it badly. I think that's safest. But I'd like to know why you said yes to me."

"Anwen said you."

"I know what Pryce said. She said it to my face; my ears rang. I'm asking you."

Kiva trod six paces to the end, and turned.

"I'd somebody else," she said. "I'd reasons. I asked Anwen, and she said you, and she was right, and I'd not have thought of you by myself." It was not flattering, and she let it stand. "Why did you say yes?"

"Because I got my first step by calling *off* twice." Pru's round, anxious face was pink with cold. "Once at a husk and once at a spaniel. The officer passed me. He said he'd sooner have a ward who ran from a dog than one who wouldn't run from anything. I've thought about that every day since." She trod. "I'd like to find out whether I can stay. Only once. Then I'll know which sort I am, and I can stop narrating it."

"You'll not stop," said Perrin.

"No. But I'd have the facts."

They wrung the sheet between four of them and hung it over two lines, where it dripped. It did not stand up in ridges. It hung like a sheet.

---

On Wednesday she chose the dear one.

That was the whole of the work, and it was harder than landing. She stood on the line with the thread cold and the step clean behind her, and she knew now that there were two roads over that strip of dirt. One of them was soft and cost a third and felt like a hand passing a cup. She had been down it once. Her body had liked it very much.

"Rowan, changing on three. Ember, to still."

"*One.*"

Up.

It was there at the top, waiting for her, like a known step on a dark stair. The metal was closing. The pan wanted setting. It would have been so little to let the one begin.

"*Two.*"

She shut the Ember, and made herself wait for it.

She waited until the last thread of metal had run home along her ribs and had arrived, and she knew that it had arrived, and there was nothing of hers open anywhere. It took no longer than a word. It was the longest thing she had ever done on purpose.

The ash went. A quarter of a dram, all at once, out of her like a breath, and she was nobody at all, in the air, over a yard, and she let herself be it.

Then the pan.

"*Three.*"

The cross. No jar. The whole of the Old Yard under one heel.

"Four." Orla's ferrule, and Orla's weight. "Five. Six. Seven. Eight."

"Give it back."

She gave it back, and shut the door.

"Thread?"

"Cold. All the way."

"Hand."

She held it out, and she knew before Orla took it. She could see it in the lamplight. The last two fingers were her own. The first two were the color of candle wax, to the second joint.

Orla did not say anything. She held the hand in both of hers, as it had not been held since a cart at a west gate, and she began to count under her breath, slowly, with her eyes on the fingertips. Nobody else in the lane made a sound.

Kiva could not feel them. She watched them lying in the old hard palm as if they had been two things that somebody had put there.

At twenty, they were white. At thirty, the first of them had a faint flush along the nail. It came up slowly from the root, like tea into a lump of sugar, and at thirty-eight she felt something that was a long way off and belonged to her.

"There," said Orla, at forty-one. She let out a breath that she had been holding rather longer than the count. "Forty-one. That's inside the sixty, and I don't care for how far inside it wasn't." She let go. "Two. I'll say that first, so that you have it. That was a crossing, and it was the dear one, and you took it with the cheap one lying there in plain sight. I saw you choose. Two."

"And we're done for the night."

"We're done for the week. E.K. wrote *not twice in one flood* and I've begun to guess at his reason." She took up her stick. "You'll show that hand to Aske tomorrow and tell him the count. If he says there's to be no go until he's seen it after a rest, then there's none, and I'll not be argued with by a girl who's just had two fingers come back from wherever they went."

"Thirty-one," said Noa, very quietly, from the mast. "Two, and twenty-six, and three."

"A dram and a quarter," said Kellan.

He had done the sum before the tape was read. He turned his notebook round on his knee, so that all of them could see the column.

*0.03. 0.30. 0.11. 0.31. Spent, 0.75. In hand, 1.25.*

"Two counted," he said. "Four to show. Four at thirty-one is a dram and twenty-four. So there's ash for four, and a hundredth." He looked up. "There isn't a miss left in it. Not a drop. Not a stack. I said on the second that it came out exact. I'd no idea how exact."

"I mind it more now that it's working," said Perrin. "I thought that I ought to say."

Kiva was flexing her left hand. The fingers closed. They did it late, and they told her nothing whatever about the palm they closed on.

Somewhere in a ledger that had been copied forward since before the Compact had a charter, there was a woman whose hand had gone to the wrist and had not come back by midsummer. She had been put on the roll as a catcher in the autumn. Kiva had thought on Tuesday that it was a hard thing to read. She thought tonight that it might have been written as a comfort, by a man who had not known how else to say that there was still work for her.

She wrote the go up at the kitchen table, with Mara reading over the top of her own book.

Then she took a half sheet and copied both nights out fair, Monday's and Wednesday's: where the line was, and what the tape said, and who had called what. She put the *why* column in. She put in the cup, and the warm thread, and a thing that a Stonehand captain had told her in a passage and that she had filed on the wrong side of the page.

She addressed it to the summer line, care of the Graywater hall. It was not a letter. It had no beginning and it was not signed, and Anwen would know the hand.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-29.md

# Chapter 29 — Kiva: The Night Van

The watch room at the Graywater hall had one wall that was all map, and one that was all sums.

They had been told six, and they were there at a quarter to, the four of them in a row on a bench with the sheet between their feet. It was a plain room. There were hooks for coats, and a stove, and a long table scarred by forty years of tin lids. On the map wall every sensing mast in the eastern districts had a small glass lamp beside its name, and every lamp was dark, and under them hung a brass bell with a cord that nobody touched.

Kellan was reading the other wall.

It was a painted board, ruled in columns, and somebody chalked it fresh each week. *District table. The season to date.* The companies went down the left side, and across the top ran five words that Kiva had last heard read out from a slate on a cinder floor, with one more added at the end.

*Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.*

"Saltmere's first," said Kellan, in the voice he kept for a sum that would not come out. "Twenty-seven calls, twenty-seven closed. Nobody lost, two hurt. Not an hour dark. Two thousand eight hundred and forty drams." His finger moved down. "Graywater, thirty-one calls. All closed. Five hurt. *Six hours dark, one branch.* Three thousand nine hundred and ten. One remark."

"That's the Pit," said Perrin. "Six hours is us."

"And third is Halden's First Company, with four thousand four hundred. They've tinned more than anybody on the wall. They've one call *held over* and two remarks, and they're third." Kellan turned round. "Graywater's eleven hundred drams ahead of Saltmere, and it stands behind. How is the table reckoned?"

"From the left."

The man who had spoken was sitting at the end of the long table, mending a cord. He was square and grey-headed and he had been there when they came in. Kiva had known him at once by his back. She had last seen it on a meadow, four paces from a headless Flask, not turning round.

"You read it as you'd read a line of print," he said. He did not look up from the cord. "Was it closed? If one company's closed them all and another hasn't, that's the end of it; you never get as far as the tin. Then lives. Then what was still working in the morning. You only come to the fourth column when the first three are level." He drew a knot tight with his teeth. "You can't buy a column with the one to its right. There's houses that keep trying."

"Then a clean yield—"

"Is the fourth thing we're for. I've been catching twenty-six years, and I'm the fourth thing we're for." He laid the cord down, finished. "Pike. You'll be Rowan. Sorn says you kneel like somebody who was taught by a tinsmith."

"By a Torch with a bad knee."

"Same thing." He looked at the bundle between their feet. "Is that wet?"

"It was trodden. An hour, in a trough."

"Who told you to?"

"A laundress."

"Then she's forgotten more about canvas than the Circuit ever knew," said Pike, and almost smiled, and the door opened.

Rhea Sorn was not as tall as the poster.

That was Kiva's first thought, and it was a stupid one. She had seen this woman on a door for six years, and across a gallery, and behind a grey column with a white line going up from her fist. She had never been in a room with her. The streak at the temple was real. The eyes were paler than they had been painted. She came in with her coat over her arm, and she looked at the four people on the bench as Mara looked at a tray of instruments that had come back from somebody else's repair.

"Paper," she said. "Each of you. What you're passed for and what you're not. Renn."

Kellan stood up. "Kill. First step, full. Second step, attached. I've cut one rung of a Flask under a foreman, and a Handful in the field."

"Cade."

"Carry. And I'm not to." Perrin got up more slowly. "Ribs. Nothing over my head, no stance under a load. The Hall's healer wrote it out and made me sign. I can take a corner and a pole. I can walk somebody out who can walk."

"Finch."

"Hold. First step." Pru's voice went up a full tone. "A corner and a lamp. Not a wall. You underlined it."

"I did. I'll say why, later, when it matters." The pale eyes came to the end of the bench. "Rowan."

It was the first word that Rhea Sorn had ever said to her.

"Catch," said Kiva. "Recovery only. Dark hands, under a declared team call. No door opened within three paces of open ash or any charged cell. I'm not certified to kill, hold, carry, or tune."

"And the other thing? I was on that floor."

"It isn't on my paper. I've two on a sanctioned mast. My sponsor wants six." She kept her hands still. "I'll not do it tonight for anybody."

"Good. I'll not ask." Rhea hung her coat on a hook. "Hollis Vane asked, and he'd a reason, and he's written it down in two books. I've read the second one; he sent it up. I've no intention of writing a third." She turned to face them, with her back to the map. "You're attached. I'll tell you what that means on my van, since the board's wording is very fine and no use. It means that you fetch, and carry, and catch behind Pike. It means you kill nothing above a Handful, and that only when I've said the word. Nairn's your officer on the ground. If he tells you to stand, you stand, and you ask him why on the way home."

A young man had come in behind her, tying back his hair. He was perhaps three-and-twenty, long in the leg, with a cheerful, sunburned face, and there was dark metal on a cuff at his wrist.

"That's me," he said. "I was you four years ago. I've not forgotten what it was like, so I'll try to be less terrible than mine was."

"You'll be exactly as terrible as is wanted," said Rhea. "Counter. Each of you settles a field dram on the company's meter before we do anything else. Then Pike will have that sheet down in the yard and up again until he's stopped wincing."

---

It took eight times before Pike stopped wincing.

He did not say what was wrong. He stood in the hall yard under a hooded lamp, with his hands behind him, and each time the sheet was down he walked round it once and said, "Up," and they took it up.

On the fifth he stopped beside Kiva.

"Your cord hand. You change your grip at the second turn."

"I've two fingers that don't tell me anything. I start in the hand, and then I have to hook the elbow to—"

"I know why. I watched you do it at the Pit, from under a string." He held out his own right hand. It was broad and scarred, and there was a groove worn across the palm. "I'd a tendon cut at thirty. That finger's been a passenger ever since. I don't ask it for anything. I took the cord the way I could hold it on my worst day, and I've held it that way every day since, the good ones too." He closed the hand. "A grip you change is two grips, with a moment between when you've got neither. You know about moments between. I'm told that you pay a quarter of a dram a time for one."

Kiva looked at him.

"So choose the bad-day grip. Elbow from the first turn, not the second. It's slower by a count. Take the count." He walked on. "Up."

She took the cord at the elbow from the first turn on the sixth throw, and the seventh, and the eighth. It was slower by a count. It felt like wearing somebody else's boots. On the eighth, Pike went all the way round the sheet and said nothing whatever, which she understood to be the most that anybody got.

After that there was nothing to do, and Kiva learned that this was most of the trade.

They sat. The stove ticked. Four ward-holders in blue played a slow game of cards at the far end of the table, and one of them was Imrie, who had stood in a line at the Pit and had not once looked round. Nairn slept in a chair with his boots on a second chair. A bald man called Ruddock, who drove the van and was the crew's healer, went through a satchel of linen and counted it twice.

At twenty minutes to ten the bell rang.

It rang two strokes and a pause, and two again, and a lamp came up on the map wall, out on the edge of the allotments. Nobody ran. The cards were laid face down. Nairn's eyes opened. Within about ninety seconds twelve people and their kit were in the back of a long blue van on two facing benches, and the hall doors were open on the Brickfields road.

"Sag Line, mast five," said Rhea, from the front. "That's a recalibrated mast, on a baseline that's been creeping all spring. It's rung false twice this month. We go as though it hadn't."

It had.

There was nothing on the Sag but a wind and a smell of turned earth. They stood on the berm for twenty minutes in the dark while Pike walked the cut with a hooded lantern and Rhea watched the place where a tear might have been. Then she took a slate from her coat and wrote on it, and read it out, in the voice of somebody reading a laundry list.

"*Closed: nothing open. Hurt: none. Works: all. Tinned: nil.* That's a call. It's on the table by morning, the same as the Pit."

Down the berm, where the big Ferren culvert came out under the dyke, there was a light.

It was a small one, the silver of a held ward, steady as a lamp in a window. Two men in Works oilskins were going into the culvert's mouth by it with a measuring rod. Behind them stood a row of people in blue with their hands at their sides, and at the end of the row, a little apart, a figure with both arms raised was holding the light up for the men to see by.

Kiva knew the way she stood.

She was sixty paces off, across a drainage cut. The light did not waver. It was not a wall and it was not holding anything back; it was only a lamp, lifted so that two inspectors should not put a foot wrong in the dark. She had been given that in her second week, and Kiva did not know enough to tell whether it was early.

Anwen did not look round. She could not have told one dark shape on a berm from another, and Kiva did not call. It was Mace's line. They had come about a mast.

"Somebody you know," said Rhea Sorn. She had come up beside Kiva without a sound.

"She was our Hold."

"I know who she is. I've read her card as well." The pale eyes were on the small steady light. "That's a good lamp. He'll have a wall on her inside the month." A pause. "Van."

She asked them on the way back. The van hummed along the embankment, and she turned on the front bench with one arm along its back.

"There was nothing there. Suppose there had been. One husk, plain, in that cut. Renn. What do you do?"

"Ask my catcher for the mark. Come at it dark. Light after the foot." Kellan answered as he would have answered the Chair. "I'd expect to have it bagged inside forty-five."

"Rowan?"

"Read the wind and the ground. Set the cloth in the lee of the berm. Call *dark* at the cut."

"Finch?"

"Stand where I'm put and light when I'm told and drop when she says." Pru considered. "And narrate, I expect. Quietly."

"Cade."

"Find out who can't walk."

"Mm." Rhea looked at them for a moment longer. "There's an allotment pump forty paces east of that cut. Agnes Roake's. A team put its cell out of order in the spring with a four-second kill on that very bank, and she waited six weeks for the reserve to fill it." She let that lie. "You've each told me what you'd do. They were good answers. Not one of you has told me what's behind you."

Nobody said anything. The van went on through the dark.

"I'll not ask it again tonight," said Rhea, and turned to the front.

---

Anwen Pryce heard the second bell from the mouth of a culvert, at fourteen minutes past two.

It came across the flats from the east, very small and clear, three strokes and a pause and three again. She had learned the codes in her first week. Three and three was the weigh-house.

"Weighbridge," said somebody along the line.

"Not ours," said Mace. He did not turn his head. "Night van's. We're lamps. Pryce, you're dipping."

She was. She brought it up. It was the fifth hour she had held a light over a brick mouth for two men with a rod, and her arms had stopped being arms a long while ago and become two things that she balanced a lamp on. *A wall takes it out of your wrists,* Stroud had told her, with her own hands in linen. *A lamp takes it out of your patience. He'll leave you on a lamp till he's seen which you run out of first.*

The night van. She had seen it at ten, up on the berm, a long dark shape with its lamps hooded, and a row of people beside it against the sky. She had not been able to tell one from another. She had known all the same.

They would be on the benches now, with the sheet between their feet. Finch would be talking. Kellan would have a watch out. Perrin would be saying that he minded something, to keep from saying what he was afraid of.

There was a half sheet in her coat, under the list.

It had come up by Mace's runner on Thursday, with no beginning and no name at the foot. She had read it standing in a culvert in the rain. *Monday. Why: it was easy and I was pleased, and I didn't look at my feet.* And lower, under a ruled line, in the same square pencil: *A thing L. Brask said in a passage. I put it on the left. It belonged on the right.*

She had read that six times. It was not an apology. It had none of the roundness of one. It was only the counts, as she had asked for them, with the part left in that made the writer look a fool.

She was still angry. She had examined it that night as she would have examined a ward for a thin place, and it was all there, as sound as the day she had turned her list over on a kitchen table. Greta Stroud could hold a spoon now, and not a cup.

And she wanted very badly to be on that van.

It seemed that a person could have both of those at once and hold a lamp. Nobody had told her so. She had thought that when she finally stood on a Graywater line she would be one whole thing, with nothing left over.

She had learned a good many things in a fortnight that nobody had told her. A culvert at night has a tide in it, of air, and a lamp held against that draft wants the weight on the back foot. The oldest hand on the line ate a raw onion at midnight and could stand four hours on it. Mace watched feet, and never hands.

On Thursday he had stood behind her through the whole of a relief with his board under his arm. At the end of it he had said the first thing that he had ever said to her before seven in the morning.

*Tuesday. You'll take the end of the wall at the low arch, behind nobody. If it dips, I'll hear it.*

That was the second week, and not the fourth. She had not told anybody. There was nobody on the line that it would have been manners to tell, and the one person whom she would once have told before her own mother was on a bench in a van tonight. That person had been informed, across a fold of waxed canvas, that Anwen was not done with her.

She did not regret it. She found that she could want to tell somebody a thing and still not be done.

The bell had stopped. Whatever it was had been heard by the people whose business it was.

"There's the low arch, miss," said the inspector, from inside the brick. "A foot to your left, if you'd be so good."

She moved the light a foot to the left, and held it there, and it did not flicker.

---

The van took the turn off the tram road at twenty-seven minutes past two, and stopped with its nose to the top of a street that had no lamps in it.

"Weighbridge Street," said Nairn, and dropped the tailboard.

Kiva had been down it once or twice by day. It was an ordinary street. It ran south for a hundred and fifty paces and fell a little all the way, between two rows of yellow brick tenements five stories high, to a wharf on the canal. There were railings, and front steps, and iron gratings set into the pavements over the cellars. At the bottom stood the old weigh-house with the mast on its roof. Beyond it, across the water, the Lantern lay along the far bank in a long low wash of steady white light, as it had lain every night for four hundred years.

On this side, every window was dark.

It was not an empty dark. She could feel that as she got down. There were people behind those windows, four or five hundred of them, in back rooms with their doors shut and their hands in their laps, and not one had so much as a candle. A woman's voice was calling something down a stairwell, flat and steady, a floor at a time.

"They've done a dark drill," said Ruddock, pulling out the satchel. "The wardens ring it on the bell. Lamps out, doors shut, nobody at the front. This street's had the same drill since before there were trams." He nodded at the high wall on the right, where a pair of iron gates stood open on a yard full of sleeping cars. "And that's the sheds."

"That's my father's shed," said Perrin.

He said it quite quietly. There were five or six people at the gates, in leather aprons, standing well back in the shadow with their arms folded, and one of them was a short, wide man with a grey mustache who had lifted his hand about an inch when the van stopped and put it down again.

"Is he on nights?" said Kiva.

"He's always on nights. He says the cars are better company." Perrin had gone a curious color. "He's never seen me on a floor that wasn't behind glass."

Just inside the gates a woman sat on an upturned crate with a child asleep against her shoulder, and a man in his shirt stood over the pair of them with a blanket he had not thought to unfold. They had come out of a ground-floor window by the back. Somebody from the sheds had given them a mug of tea, and none of the three had touched it.

A lamp warden came up out of the dark with a dead lantern in his hand. He had the street's switch already pulled, he said, and the wardens rung. Two were out. The first had come with the bell, at fourteen minutes past, and gone in at a ground-floor window halfway down on the west side, and the family had got out the back and pulled their red handle as they went. The second had come at twenty-four past. It was sitting on the Hythe Building's step.

"Who's in this street that can't be got out by the back?" said Rhea.

"Two that I know of. There's an old carter at the top of the Hythe on a breathing bellows; he's not been down those stairs in a year. And a child in the Pellow, two up, at the front, whose physic has to be kept cold. The mother wouldn't leave the cupboard. Warden Cobb's shut her in the back room with it." The lamp warden rubbed his jaw. "They're both on the cores, ma'am. The cell boxes are only the reserve."

"Until a core trips. Then they're on a box the size of a biscuit tin." Rhea's eyes went down the dark fronts. "That's two more things behind us. And the thing itself?"

"Below the Pellow. Two-thirds down, plumb in the middle." He pointed with the dead lantern at a tall front on the east side, with a crack over its door that somebody had stapled long ago with iron. "It's kept its time so far, ma'am. One, and ten minutes, and one."

"So far," said Rhea.

She stood at the top of the street with her hands in her pockets and looked down it for about as long as it would have taken to count twenty. Kiva looked where she looked. The cobbles ran away downhill, grey in the shine from the far bank. Two-thirds of the way down, in the very middle of the roadway, there was a place where the dark was the wrong color. It was a thin upright stroke of blue, taller than a man, like the gap at the edge of a door.

"Listen," said Rhea. She did not raise her voice, and twelve people heard her. "This is a Handful tear, in a street with a core under every doorstep and a cell box on every kitchen wall. That's what's behind you. So nothing dies within reach of a grating, and nothing's lit that I haven't put there. The reason is that every one of those cellars is a lamp."

She took her hands out of her pockets.

"Mine's the one on the step. Imrie lights for it at the Hythe; Pike has the cloth. Line stands dark at the forty, in case it gives us something that isn't a Handful. Seven." The pale eyes came round. "The one on the west pavement is inside your step. Cloth, lure, cut, catch, as you were passed. In the middle of the road, and no nearer a railing than you can help. Nairn stands over you. Go."

---

They went down the west pavement in file, past the Hythe, and Kiva saw how the others began.

Imrie had walked out onto the crown of the road and stopped there with her hands at her sides. Pike had a sheet under his arm. He did not throw it. He stood for a moment and turned his square head slowly the whole way round, up the hill and down it and along both rows of dark fronts, as a man looks about a room before he sits. Then the canvas went down, and he knelt, and only then did the silver come up between Imrie's palms.

Kiva thought that he was being slow. She had a husk of her own, and a mark to find for it, and she did not think of it again for a quarter of an hour.

She set her mark by the crown of the road.

She knelt and put her palm on the cobbles. They fell away south, and a little to either gutter. There was no wind. The air lay in the street like water in a trough. She chose the high middle, fourteen paces above the blue stroke, where the stone was dry and nothing sloped toward a grating.

"Here. Pru, two paces down from the south hem. On my word."

The sheet went down. It lay as Pike had made it lie, flat, at the first throw.

The husk was on the west pavement, eight or nine paces off, with its blunt head pushed through the area railings of a house whose ground-floor window was only a frame. It was the size of a big dog and the grey of a wet slate. It was working at a grating, patiently, as a dog works at a rabbit hole.

"Lure," said Kiva.

Pru's hands came up. The ward that stood between them was the size of a tea tray, and it hummed.

The blunt head came out of the railings. It turned, and it came straight, down off the curb and across the cobbles at a trot, as they always came, with nothing in it but direction. Kiva did not watch it. She watched the hem. She heard Kellan go past her on his struts, dark, and she heard his foot come down.

There was a line of blue-white light, as long as a breath.

"Dark!" said Kiva. "One."

Pru's ward went out like a pinched wick. Twenty-odd drams lay on the middle of the canvas, as soft as flour.

It went as it had gone on a dozen floors. Corners up at five. Perrin on the west hem with his arms held stiff, Kellan north, Pru south. The neck came in. The cord went round at her hooked elbow, slower by a count, once, and twice, and at the third turn her two dull fingers told her nothing whatever about the cross and she said, "Cord check," and Pru said, "Crossed right. Over, then under toward you," and it was.

"Sealed. Forty-one."

"Tidy," said Nairn. He had stood three paces off through all of it with his arms folded. "That's tidier than mine was at twenty. Get it on a pole."

Up the street, at the Hythe's step, there had been a longer line of light, and a white one. Kiva had seen it at the edge of her eye. She had heard a flat voice say *thirty-three.*

"Forty-one," said Kellan, low. He was unhooking the weights with quick fingers, and his eyes were bright. "Theirs was thirty-three. With a Torch, and a man who's done it nine hundred times. We could be under forty on the next. Did you see how it lay? It never lifted."

"I saw."

She had. She was pleased. She knew it for what it was, and gave it a number, and it went on sitting in her chest like a warm stone all the same.

Perrin took the bag on a pole across his shoulders and started up the hill toward the van at a careful walk. The sheet lay open on the crown of the road. Pru was blowing on her hands.

Ten minutes, the warden had said. *It's kept its time.* The next was not due until twenty-six minutes to three.

Down the street, the blue stroke opened a little, like an eye, and a third husk stepped out of it onto the cobbles.

It was two minutes early.

"There," said Kellan. "That's ours. Same mark. It's fourteen paces from the cloth and the cloth's down."

Nairn had his back to them. He had turned to look up the hill, to where a woman with a white streak in her hair was standing in the roadway with one hand raised, flat, at the height of her shoulder. He began to say something. It began with *wait.*

Kiva looked at the sheet.

It was down, and it was flat. The ground was the same ground. The air had not moved. Three minutes ago she had set a lure two paces below that hem and a Handful had come across the road to it like a tram on a rail, and they had sealed at forty-one.

"Cloth's down," she said. "Pru. Lure."

The tea tray came up, humming.

The third husk turned its head toward it.

And behind the husk, the blue opened as a mouth opens.

A fourth came through at a scramble, sideways, with a shine on its shoulders. And after it, before it was clear, something began to rise out of the gap that was not the size of a dog. It came up a knot at a time, as a rope is paid out of a hatch: a grey bulk, and another above it, and another, on legs as thick as lampposts, until its blunt top stood level with the second-floor sills and there was frost on it from crown to knee.

It did not look about. It had nothing to look with. The top knot swung, slowly, through a quarter of a circle, as a weathercock swings when the wind comes round.

It stopped on a tea tray.

"*GATE!*"

That was Rhea. Kiva had never heard her shout. Up the hill four people were running, and their boots were loud on the stones.

"Seven, *dark!*"

"Pru!" said Kiva. "Dark, dark—"

The ward went out. Pru was good at that; it was the thing she was best at in the world. It was out before the second word.

But the Flask had already lifted a leg.

Kiva saw it come. It came over the south hem of her own sheet, a grey column against the shine from the far bank, quite slowly, with all the time there was. It was coming down on the place where the light had been. Pru was standing in that place with her hands still raised and her mouth open, and she was not narrating anything.

Something hit her from the side.

It was Nairn. He took her at the waist with his shoulder, running, and she went over like a ninepin and slid on her back across the wet canvas. He went down on one knee with the force of it.

The leg came down.

There was a sound. Kiva had heard a brake shoe let go once, in a tram yard, when she was small. It was like that, and a good deal shorter.

Then light broke across the street behind her, silver and enormous, and the top knot lifted off the place where Pru had been and went to it. The Flask took one step north. It took another. Its blunt head came to rest against the gate, eight paces up the hill, and it leaned there, and was still.

It was standing over them.

Kiva was on her knees at the north hem with six grey legs round her like the posts of a pier. The lowest knot hung an arm's length above her head, slack and heavy, turning a little. She could feel the cold coming off it. Kellan lay flat on the cobbles with both hands spread. Pru was on her back on the sheet, staring straight up.

Nairn was beside the south hem. He had got up onto his elbows. One of his legs was under him, and the other went away beneath a column of grey as thick as a barrel, and did not come out the far side.

He was not making any noise. His face was the color of the canvas.

"Nobody," he said, quite clearly, "light anything."

Nobody did.

The third husk trotted past them up the hill, to the wall. The fourth had not followed. Kiva heard its feet on the pavement below her, quick and light, going toward a door with a crack over it.

She knelt under a Flask with her hands flat on her own new sheet. Three drams, and four signatures. It had lain so well.

*Lure,* she had said. She had read the wind and the fall of the ground. She had not left one thing out that a catcher is taught to look for. It had not once crossed her mind to ask who else in that street might be able to see a light.

*Not one of you has told me what's behind you.*

Up the hill, behind the silver, a pair of boots was coming down at a walk. It was a heavy, careful walk, the walk of somebody who has been forbidden to run and has dropped whatever he was carrying.

"That's a remark," said Rhea Sorn's voice, from somewhere beyond the legs. It was perfectly level. "It goes on the table, and it's mine. Seven, stay as you are. Don't speak. I'm coming round."

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-2680-opening/manuscript/chapter-30.md

# Chapter 30 — Kiva: What's Behind You

Rhea Sorn came round the legs of the Flask as a person comes round a parked wagon in a narrow lane, sideways, with her coat held in.

She looked at Nairn first. She looked for about a second, and then she crouched and put two fingers on the cobbles beside his hip, well clear of the grey.

"Below the knee?"

"Below. I felt it go." His voice was thin and careful. "It's standing on the weight. There's a lead weight in that hem and my shin's between the two."

"Then you'll keep still, and you'll not be brave." She stood. "Seven. Faces to me. Is anybody else under anything?"

"No," said Kiva.

"Then you may stand, slowly, and come out by the west side. Not Finch. Finch, crawl. Keep off your hands; there's wax on that sheet."

They came out. Kiva had not known how much she was shaking until she tried to walk. The Flask did not stir. Its blunt top lay against the silver eight paces up the hill, and beside it, small as a dog at a gate, the third husk stood with its head pressed to the wall.

Down the street there was a sound of iron.

It was a railing, being worked at. Kiva turned. A dozen paces below, on the east pavement, a grey shape with a shine on its shoulders had got its head and one forelimb through the area bars of the house with the cracked lintel, and was reaching for the window beneath the front step. There was a board across that window on the inside. Somebody had put it there in a hurry.

"That one's going in," said Rhea.

She said it to nobody. Then she turned her head a little, so that the four of them and the man on the ground could hear, and she spoke as Hollis Vane had spoken on a cinder floor, with the same flat care.

"There's a ground-floor front behind that board with people in it. I've no cloth that I can get at and no time to lay one. I'm going to cut it where it stands, on a pavement, over a cellar grating. The ash will go down the grating. It'll foul that building's core, and likely more." She was already moving. "A window before a cellar. That's my trade, and it goes on the slate under my name."

She did not run. She went down the east curb with long, even strides, and stopped a pace behind the thing at the railings, where its shine could not come round to her.

Her left foot came down. Her right came down.

The line that went up from her fist was white, and no wider than a finger, and it was there for less time than it takes to blink.

The husk fell in through its own shoulders. Four-and-twenty drams of open ash poured down the front of the area steps, soft and quick, and Kiva saw it lean as it fell. It did not lie where it landed. It went to the grating as water goes to a drain.

There was a count of perhaps five.

Then, from somewhere under the street, came a deep, flat *clunk* that she felt in the soles of her feet. She had felt that once before, at a quarter to three in the morning, in a hallway on Coper Street.

Inside the house with the cracked lintel a handbell began to ring, hard and steady.

"*Pellow!*" It was a woman's voice, an old one, coming down a stairwell. "Core's down! You're on your boxes! Nobody touches a red handle but me!"

Rhea was walking back up the hill. Her face showed nothing whatever.

"That's the Pellow on reserve," she said. "Eight-and-forty flats. Now. I've a blade under a Flask." She stopped beside Nairn. "I can't cut what's on him; the legs go last, and if I take the top off this now, sixty drams come down on a man who can't crawl out from under it. I can't walk the gate; the thing would follow it straight across him. I want that leg up a hand's breadth for the count of five, and there isn't a still-door on my van."

Perrin had come down the pavement while she spoke. He stood with his arms held away from his ribs, breathing through his mouth.

"There's one here," he said.

"I've read your healer's paper."

"So have I. I signed it. I can't, and I'm not going to say that I can; I'd fold in the middle and then you'd have two." He said it steadily, though it plainly cost him. "But there are three up at that gate who lift tram bogies for their living. My father's a still-door. He never went near a Hall. He'd tell you that he only stands under things."

Rhea looked at him for the space of a breath.

"Can you teach a man to give a load back, in a minute, in the dark?"

"I can teach that one. He taught me to ease a brake."

"Fetch them. Dark, at a walk, on the west pavement." She turned. "Renn. You carried a woman off a floor on the twenty-ninth with nothing lit. Say it."

"Renn, Seven. Carry."

"Ruddock's at the curb with a board. You take him under the arms when I say *now* and not before, and you don't stop for any noise he makes. Rowan, Finch. You're the road. I want that pole and that cord and every weight you can reach out of his way. Dark hands. Go."

---

Kiva knelt beside Nairn in the cold that came down off the lowest knot.

The cord lay across his hip. She drew it clear, a hand's width at a time, and passed it back to Pru. She unhooked the weight at the south-west corner and slid it away over the cobbles. The one that mattered she could not reach. It was under the edge of the grey, and his leg was on it.

"You're being rescued," she said. "In about a minute. On a count."

"Is that Cade's line?" His eyes were shut. "He told me in the van. He says he likes to be told."

"He lends it out."

"Tell him it's a comfort. It isn't, but tell him." A breath. "I'd my back to you. I was looking for her hand. I ought to have had my eyes on the three of you and I'd them up the hill, like a first-year waiting to be nodded at." His mouth moved. "So don't put all of this in your book. Some of it's mine."

"I called the lure."

"You did. I'd have called it too, at seventeen. I did, once, on the river flats, and a man called Ostrey lost two teeth." His hand found the hem of the sheet and gripped it. "It lay very well, your canvas. I noticed that."

There were boots on the pavement.

Three people in leather aprons came down out of the dark behind Perrin, a short wide man with a grey mustache, a tall woman with her sleeves rolled, and a thick-necked young man who kept looking up at the knots and then at his feet. They stopped where Perrin stopped. None of them said a word. They had the stillness of people who work at night beside heavy things that can move.

"That's a leg," said Perrin. He pointed with his chin. "It's nothing but weight. You've had a bogie come off the jack in pit three, Dad; you told me. It's that. You're not lifting it. Nobody could. You're getting in under it and letting the floor have it."

"Under where?" said his father. His voice was deep and quiet.

"There. A pace above the lad's knee, where it thickens. Three of you, shoulder on, like the pit props. Dulcie on the outside; you're tallest." Perrin wet his lips. "Open your stance when I say and not before. Then there are two words. On *give* you ease off, like easing a shoe. You'll feel it settle onto you. On *take* you stand up into it, all at once, and you let it go straight down through your boots. Don't hold it in your back. Your back's got nothing to do with it. It's the street that's strong."

"Give, and take."

"It's a school's count. I had it off a man who had it off them." He glanced at Kiva. "It works on a lid."

"The moment the three of you light," said Rhea, "that thing may turn its head to see what's behind it. If it does, my line takes it back. You'll have four counts, and you'll use them to finish what you're doing. You don't look up."

"We don't generally," said the tall woman.

They went in. Kiva moved back to give them room. She saw Perrin's father set his shoulder to the grey as he might have set it to the frame of a car, and frown, and shift his feet a few inches wider on the wet canvas.

"On three," said Perrin. "One. Two. Three. *Open.*"

It was not like the Hall. There was no ring to it. Three ordinary still-doors came open in the dark, as dull as stove lids, and she felt them through the cobbles, the plain heaviness of people who had never been measured.

Above her the lowest knot turned a quarter, slowly, and stopped.

"*Give.*"

The leg settled. It came down onto three shoulders with a creak that was not a sound a living thing makes.

"*Take!*"

They stood up into it.

It rose. It rose about the width of a hand, and held there, trembling, with Nairn's shin lying under it in the wrong shape.

"*Now,*" said Rhea.

Kellan had him under the arms. He went backward off the sheet in four long steps, bent double, and Nairn made one noise, a short one, with his teeth shut. Then there was a board, and Ruddock's bald head, and a satchel coming open.

"He's out," said Kiva.

"Then give it back," said Perrin. His voice shook. "Slowly, Dad. It isn't a wrench. Let the ground have it a bit at a time, and then walk out from under."

The leg came down onto the lead weight. The three of them stepped clear, one after another, and shut what they had opened, and stood in the roadway looking at their own hands.

The top knot had not left the wall.

Perrin's father rubbed his shoulder. He looked up at the grey column, the whole height of it, and then at his son, standing on the curb with his arms out from his sides like a man in a barrel.

"You never lifted it," he said.

"No."

"They've you strapped."

"To the armpits. I'm not let."

"And you said so. In front of her." The grey mustache moved. "Good lad. That's the first sensible thing I've known a Hall to teach anybody. I'd thought it was all plates." He put his cap on. "Does the lady want us for anything else? We've a quiet night up there. All the cars are asleep."

---

Rhea Sorn wanted them for a good deal, and she had about a minute in which to decide what.

She stood on the west curb and made herself look at all of it at once. That was the trade. Anybody could look at a Flask.

*What's there?*

A string on her gate, eight paces above the place where it had come out. Four wards on that gate, and they had been lit four minutes; Imrie's forearms were already steaming. One Handful, plain, butting the wall at the west end. A tear twenty paces down that had stopped keeping time. A tripped core under forty-eight flats, and a child's physic in a cupboard that was now running on a box the size of a biscuit tin.

One blade, where there had been two. Ruddock said both bones. Nairn would walk in the autumn.

And four students on a curb, who had done exactly what a qualifier had taught them, three minutes after it had been praised.

*Whose is that?*

She had asked them in the van what was behind them. She had liked the question. She had thought it rather a neat one, and she had let it lie there for them to pick up, and had turned to the front. *I'll not ask it again tonight.* She had said that. She had underlined three words on a card and promised to say why later, when it mattered.

It had mattered at about twenty-eight minutes to three. She had been twenty paces up a hill with her hand raised.

*Mine,* thought Rhea. *Then say it properly.*

"Everything in this street stays dark but that wall," she said. She did not raise her voice. "Now. Roles. Every one of us, and mine first, aloud, with the reason, as though nobody here had ever done it before. We've just learned what happens when I take it as read."

They had come round her. The four students. Pike. Three people in aprons who had not been told to go.

"Sorn. Kill. But not here. This street's a row of lamps with brick over them, and I'll not open sixty drams eight times between two lines of gratings. The wharf has no cellars. I cut there." She turned her head. "Imrie's four. Hold. Pike. Catch, at the wharf, with seven sheets and the last one doubled. Rowan catches second, turn about with him, on her own count. That's her paper to the letter. The reason's the clock: two catchers halve it, and there's a building on its boxes."

"Second," said the girl. She had gone white, and her voice held.

"Renn. Corners, for whichever of them is kneeling. You've nothing here to cut and you'll not be asked." He took it without a flicker; she noted that. "Cade. You go up the Pellow's stair to the warden, at a walk. You find me the child at the second-floor front and you tell me what that cupboard's running on and how long it's got. You're the only one here who can be spared to be slow."

"And us?" said the man with the mustache.

"Can you keep a door shut for sixty counts, and hold the corner of a sheet, and do what a square man tells you?"

"It's most of what I do."

"Then you're corners, the three of you. Wharf. Go with Pike." She breathed. "That leaves the Handful on my wall, and the turn."

The Handful she dealt with first, because it was simple, and because it was going to cost her.

It stood a pace from the west end of the gate with its head against the silver. There was no skin on it. She could take it from any side. And wherever she took it, twenty-four drams would fall at the foot of a lit wall, inside the reach of four wards that could not go dark for one count without a Flask walking off to find a cellar.

"I'm going to print that," she said. "On my own line's light. It'll be salted, every dram. That's a Handful of clean tin gone for the sake of a clear gate, and I'd have it written that I chose it."

Nobody answered. She did it. It took four-tenths of a second, and afterward a low grey heap lay along the foot of the wall with a thread of silver running through it, as bright as a hair.

Now the turn.

The gate stood uphill of the string. The wharf lay down. To lead a Flask south she wanted a light to the south of it, inside twenty-five paces, and nearer than anything else it could read. She had four wards, and all four were under its chin. If she split them two and two, each half would be a wall that a child could lean through.

A fifth would have done it. Stroud would have done it without being asked, and Stroud was at home with a spoon. The Pryce girl would have done it, and she was holding a lamp over a drain on the Sag, since Rhea's own company had sent her there, and rightly.

*A corner and a lamp. Not a wall.*

She looked at the curb.

The first-year was sitting on it with her arms round her knees and wax all down her back. She was not talking. Rhea had been told that she talked without stopping, and she had not said a word since a leg came down.

"Finch."

The round face came up.

"I underlined three words on a card. Tell me why."

"Because a wall has to hold something." It was a very small voice. "And I can't."

"And a lamp?"

"Only has to be seen."

"By whom?"

The girl's mouth opened and stayed open. She looked at the grey column, and then down the street at the blue stroke, and Rhea watched her do the sum that she should have been made to do in a van.

"That's what I didn't ask," said Finch. "I lit it for the husk. Twelve paces. I never asked who else." She swallowed. "That reads me. From twenty. Twenty-five. Anything in the gap reads me inside twelve. And nothing else in this street's alight, so I'd be the nearest. I'd be the only."

"Then say it as you'd declare it."

"Finch. Seven. Lamp." Her chin was shaking. "For the Flask, from twenty paces, and I don't know for how long."

"For about a hundred counts. You'll go down the east pavement, dark, till you're level with that door with the crack. You'll stand in the road and light. When it turns to you, you'll walk backward down the hill and it will follow, and you will never once let it come nearer than four paces, because a lamp can't be leaned on. My line runs round by the west and makes a gate below you. When Imrie says *mine,* you drop. That's the whole of it." Rhea held her eye. "Your file says that you put out a wall on your own count in front of two schools. That's why your name's on my card. It wasn't for what you can hold."

"I can't walk backward downhill past that. I'll look round. I know I shall."

"You'll not need to. I'll have you by the belt." Rhea heard herself say it, and it was simply true. "I'm what's behind you. That's my job tonight, and I'd let it slip. You look at the knot and you count your paces aloud, and you leave the street to me."

It was the longest hundred counts that she could remember.

The girl lit at the Pellow's door. The tea tray hummed between her hands. Up the hill, on a word, four wards went dark together, and the Flask stood for a moment with its blunt head leaning on nothing.

Then the top knot swung round.

"It's seen me," said Finch. "It's turned. It's lifting a—it's coming. Eight paces. That's seven. I'm going back. Step. Step."

"Left a little," said Rhea. She had two fingers through the back of the girl's belt and her own eyes on the cobbles. "There's a grating. Now straight."

"Six paces. Six. It doesn't hurry, does it? I'd thought it would. Step."

They went down past the Pellow. A grey spill lay across the pavement there and over the area step, gone dull, and Rhea looked at it as she passed. Four-and-twenty drams. A board at a window, and behind it a bell still ringing.

The tear was on her right hand now. They were going by it at arm's length, on the east side, with two paces of road to spare. She could feel it on her cheek. It was colder than the night, and it made no sound at all.

"Five paces. I've let it get to five. Is that the blue? I can see blue. I'm not looking."

"You're not looking. Step. It's behind us. Step."

"I'm narrating."

"I know. Go on. It's the best count I've had all night."

Twenty paces below, four people ran out into the roadway from the west gutter and turned, and the street filled with silver.

"*Mine,*" said Imrie's voice.

The tea tray went out. It went out between one syllable and the next, clean, with nothing left hanging.

Rhea drew her back against a railing, with two fingers still in the belt, and watched the top knot. It had been on a lamp. Now it was on a wall. It took its next step without so much as a check, and went by them at four paces' distance down the hill, and she had not heard the join.

"Was that it?" said Finch. "Was that staying?"

"That was staying." Rhea let go of the belt. The blue stroke hung in the road behind them, and as she looked, it widened by a finger. "And here's another. Seven!"

---

Kiva had the team's sheet half rolled when she heard her team called.

It had taken the two of them to drag it clear of where the Flask had stood. She let go of it and turned, and Kellan turned beside her.

A husk had come out of the tear. It stood a dozen paces below them on the crown of the road, a plain one, the grey of slate, with nothing lit anywhere near it. Its head went slowly from side to side.

"Seven!" Rhea was thirty paces down the hill and going farther, at the tail of the gate. "That's inside your step, and it's yours. I've nobody else. *Tell me how before you touch it.* Loud."

There were two of them. Pru was a lamp. Perrin was on a stair. A team that did three of the four had failed its response, and they had one and a half.

"Corners," Kiva said to Kellan. "We want two that'll stay dark."

"The shed hands. That pair." He was already beckoning. The tall woman and the thick-necked young man had stopped on the west pavement with one of Pike's sheets between them. "And a lure, and we've no ward."

"A blade's a lamp. You've said so."

"I have." His eyes came round to her. "Who else reads it?"

It was strange to hear it asked. It was strange how much room there was in a street, once it had been.
